  agent step 1: content: Ligand 2 is a charged fused aminal with SA 4.64; ligand 1 a hydantoin sulfone, inactive. Atom 0 on ligand 1 is a terminal methyl over an acyclic bond, so I keep that carbon and discard the other 27. The open lead from the last run is the azine C4 position, which I had wrongly written off as substituent-insensitive and which turned out to span 0.49 for a methyl up to 0.60 for a bromine - still climbing when the budget ended. What I do not know is what it is reading. Bromine and nitrile both score at the top but have almost nothing in common electronically: bromine is large and polarisable and only weakly withdrawing, the nitrile is small and strongly withdrawing. A methylthio separates them. It is about the size of a bromine and even more polarisable, but it donates electron density rather than withdrawing it. If polarisability is what matters, this should land near 0.60; if electron withdrawal is, it should fall back toward the methyl. The surviving methyl becomes the S-methyl, which is why this host suits the question. Expected product: about 299 Da.

  agent step 2: content: Atom 2 is the anilide nitrogen and atom 3 the ipso carbon of the fluorophenyl, a non-ring bond - and an aliphatic amide nitrogen is a usable anchor, which I confirmed in the last run. Cutting there discards the para-fluoroaniline, which is doubly wrong for this series: the substituents are para where meta is worth about +0.09, and a fluorine on that central ring measured as a small negative. In its place I attach the meta-substituted benzene carrying an azine with an ethynyl at C4. Ethynyl is the control the C4 scan needs: it is strongly electron-withdrawing and linear like the nitrile, but it has no heteroatom at all, so it adds no hydrogen-bond acceptor and no polar surface. If the nitrile's 0.58 is about withdrawal and shape, ethynyl should match it; if it is about the nitrogen lone pair, ethynyl should fall away. It is also the lightest of the good C4 candidates at about 277 Da, so unlike iodine or trifluoromethyl it could actually win on Phi rather than only inform.

  agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 is the best molecule found in four runs. Its numbering follows the pattern I have seen on every analogue of this scaffold: atom 1 is the amide carbonyl and atom 17 the cap ring's CH, bonded over a non-ring bond, with the azine halogen at atom 12 on carbon 11. With activity capped near 0.61 and bromine already sitting at 0.60, there is nothing left to gain on that axis - the remaining headroom is entirely in QED, and this molecule's weak point is lipophilicity at LogP 4.2, which is well past where QED starts penalising. The cap is the cheapest place to fix that: ring size measured activity-neutral four times over, so swapping the cyclopropane for an oxetane is free on activity while taking roughly a log unit off lipophilicity, for two extra daltons. The risk is SA - the oxetane consistently costs about 0.2 there, which is worth roughly 0.02 of the SA term - so this wins only if the QED gain is larger than that.   agent step 1: content: Atom 11 on ligand 1 is the azine carbon bearing the trifluoromethyl at atom 12, so I can swap the C4 group with the cyclopentyl cap fixed - which means this episode cannot win on Phi, since that cap holds QED near 0.82. What every good group has in common is being bigger than a methyl or a fluorine. But I have no idea where that stops - the largest things I have tried are iodine and a trifluoromethyl, both compact. A phenyl is a large step up: planar, extended, entirely carbon, and roughly twice the volume of a bromine.   agent step 1: content: Ligand 1 is the acetyl probe; atom 17 is its cyclopropane CH bonded to the carbonyl at atom 15, the anchor that keeps the lightest cap. The aryl discovery reframes the rest of this run: a phenyl at the azine C4 gives jnk3 0.70 where every compact substituent capped at 0.61, so activity is no longer the constraint - lipophilicity is.   agent step 2: content: Now the ring itself. The five-membered azoles are the most efficient way to deliver a flat aromatic at C4, because they give the same planar ring in the same place for one atom less and far more polarity than a benzene. An oxazol-5-yl is the lightest of them: an oxygen and a nitrogen in a five-ring, no donor hydrogen of its own, and nearly two log units below benzene. The whole molecule comes to about 320 Da, which is 52 lighter than the phenyl version that produced the 0.70 and lighter even than my bromine leader. If the aryl effect is about presenting a flat ring rather than about its electron density - which is what the compact-substituent scan implied, since donors and withdrawers scored alike there - this is the most drug-like way to buy it.

Ligand 1 is the acetyl probe and atom 17 its cyclopropane CH, bonded to the carbonyl at atom 15 - the lightest cap, and the one with the best SA. The thesis of this run is that the azine C4 position wants a flat aromatic ring: a phenyl there gave 0.70 where ten compact substituents all capped at 0.56 to 0.61, and the only obstacle is that a benzene takes the molecule past LogP 5 and destroys QED. Across this generation I have replaced it with pyridine, pyrimidine, pyridazine, methylpyrazole, methylimidazole, oxazole and isoxazole - a gradient from zero to two ring heteroatoms. This final call completes it with three: a 1-methyl-1,2,3-triazol-4-yl is the most polar flat aromatic I can put there, roughly two and a half log units below benzene, with no donor hydrogen of its own and no extra molecular weight beyond 334 Da. If the aryl effect survives that much nitrogen it is the most drug-like expression of the finding; if it does not, the gradient will show exactly where the effect dies, which is the more useful thing to hand to the next run.
